Mean Test with Fewer Observation than Dimension and Ratio Unbiased Estimator for Correlation Matrix
Tiefeng Jiang, Ping Li

Abstract
Hotelling’s T-squared test is a classical tool to test if the normal mean of a multivariate normal distribution is a specified one or the means of two multivariate normal means are equal. When the population dimension is higher than the sample size, the test is no longer applicable. Under this situation, in this paper we revisit the tests proposed by Srivastava and Du 2008 , who revise the Hotelling’s statistics by replacing Wishart matrices with their diagonal matrices. They show the revised statistics are asymptotically normal. We use the random matrix theory to examine their statistics again and find that their discovery is just part of the big picture. In fact, we prove that their statistics, decided by the Euclidean norm of the population correlation matrix, can go to normal, mixing chi-squared distributions and a convolution of both. Examples are provided to show the phase transition phenomenon between the normal and mixing chi-squared distributions. The second contribution of ours is a rigorous derivation of an asymptotic ratio-unbiased-estimator of the squared Euclidean norm of the correlation matrix.



1
Introduction

Among many statistical hypothesis testing problems, the Hotelling’s
T^{2}
tests
(
Hotelling 1931
)
are classic ones to study if the mean of a multivariate normal distribution is equal to the given one, or if the means of two normal distributions are equal. Let us quickly review the two problems. First, assume
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}\}
is a random sample from a
p
-dimensional normal
distribution
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, where
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is the mean vector and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is the covariance matrix. Consider the test
H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}~~\textrm{versus}~~H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\not=\boldsymbol{0}.
The Hotelling
T^{2}
test statistic is defined by
T^{2}=(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\mu)^{T}\hat{{\bf S}}^{-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}-\mu)
, where

\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}
and
\hat{{\bf S}}
are the sample mean and the sample covariance matrix. For the two population test, assume that
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{in_{i}}\}
for
i=1,2
are two independent
random samples from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, respectively. We aim to test
H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}\ \ \mbox{versus}\ \ H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}\neq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}.
The corresponding Hotelling’s
T^{2}
test statistic is given by
\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})^{T}\hat{{\bf S}}^{-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})

where
\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i}
is the sample mean vector of the
i
-th sample and
\hat{{\bf S}}
is the pooled sample covariance matrix.

As a consequence of the likelihood ratio test, the above two Hotelling
T^{2}
tests have very nice properties. For example, for the one-sample test,
T^{2}
is invariant under linear transformations. Also,
T^{2}
is a uniformly most powerful test under the general linear group; see, for example, p. 211 from
Muirhead 1982
or p. 190 from
Anderson 2003
.

Despite its important role in classical statistics, Hotelling’s
T^{2}
have some limitations. First, in order to guarantee that the sample covariance matrix is invertible, the sample size have to be larger than the population dimension. These are not true for some modern data in which the population dimension is larger or even much larger than the sample size. For example, for DNA microarray data, thousands of gene expression levels are often measured on a small number of subjects. From

http://genomics-pubs.princeton.edu/oncology/
, which is a popular colon dataset, one can see
p=2000
,
n_{1}=22
and
n_{2}=40
.
Second, as
Bai and Saranadasa 1996
have showed, the Hotelling two-sample test is inconsistent as the population dimension and the sample size are comparable.

To accommodate data with the feature of large
p
small
n
for the above one-sample and two-sample testing problems,
Dempster 1958
;
Dempster 1960
study the so-called “non-exact” test.
Bai and Saranadasa 1996
construct a new test statistic by removing the inverse matrix from the definition of
T^{2}
given earlier. Starting from this century, investigators begin to conceive new test statistics.

Srivastava and Du 2008
,
Srivastava 2009
,
Chen and Qin 2010
, and
Dong et al. 2016
replace the inverse of the sample covariance matrix by mild quantities.
Lopes et al. 2011
and
Srivastava et al. 2016
use a sort of dimension reduction method to lower the population dimension and then use Hotelling’s
T^{2}
tests. For data with a certain of sparse nature,
Cai et al. 2014
establish tests by measuring the maximum componentwise mean difference of appropriately transformed observations. Other research related to sparsity can be seen from
Zhong et al. 2013
,
Chen et al. 2014
,
Wang et al. 2015
,
Gregory et al. 2015
and
Guo and Chen 2016
. The researchers
Biswas and Ghosh 2014
,
Chang et al. 2017
,
Chakraborty and Chaudhuri 2017
, and
Xue and Yao 2020
consider the above test under non-normal assumptions. Other contributions include
Park and Ayyala 2013
and
Wu et al. 2006
for the consideration of scale-invariant tests and
Gretton et al. 2012
for a kernel-based discrepancy measure.
Feng et al. 2016
work on a two-sample location problem via a multivariate-sign-based high-dimensional test.
Xu et al. 2016
and
Zhang et al. 2020a
construct test statistics for the two-sample test problem by
l_{q}
-norm. Recently, built upon
Chen et al. 2011
, the authors
(
Li et al. 2020
)
investigate the Hotelling’s
T^{2}
by a ridge-regularized method.
Zhang et al. 2020b
extend the tests by
Wu et al. 2006
and
Zhang et al. 2020a
through a modification of the test by
Srivastava and Du 2008
.

In this paper, we will revisit the procedure by
Srivastava and Du 2008
for the one-sample and two-sample problems of testing means. We will briefly review them next and state our motivation.

Let
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}\}
be a random sample from a
p
-dimensional normal
distribution
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
. The sample mean and the sample covariance matrix defined by

\displaystyle\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \hat{{\bf S}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}})({\boldsymbol{X}}_{i}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}})^{T}.

(1)

Let
\hat{{\bf D}}
be the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
and
\hat{{\bf R}}
be the sample correlation matrix defined by
\hat{{\bf R}}=\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}\hat{{\bf S}}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}.

For the testing problem
H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}
vs
H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\not=\boldsymbol{0}
,
Srivastava and Du 2008
propose the following test statistic.

\displaystyle T_{SD}=\frac{n\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{T}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}-p(n-1)(n-3)^{-1}}{\sqrt{2[\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-p^{2}(n-1)^{-1}]}}.

(2)

Under certain conditions, they show
T_{SD}
converges to a normal distribution.

For two population case, the test is
H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}
vs
H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}\neq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}
. Assume that

\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{in_{i}}\}
for
i=1,2
are two independent
random samples from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, respectively. Let
\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i}
be the sample mean for the
i
-th sample and
\hat{{\bf S}}
be the pooled sample covariance matrix defined by

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf S}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\Big[\sum_{j=1}^{n_{1}}(\mathbb{X}_{1j}-\bar{\mathbb{X}}_{1})(\mathbb{X}_{1j}-\bar{\mathbb{X}}_{1})^{T}+\sum_{j=1}^{n_{2}}(\mathbb{X}_{2j}-\bar{\mathbb{X}}_{2})(\mathbb{X}_{2j}-\bar{\mathbb{X}}_{2})^{T}\Big].

(3)

Assume
\hat{{\bf D}}
is the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
and

\hat{{\bf R}}=\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}\hat{{\bf S}}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}
is the pooled sample correlation matrix.
Srivastava and Du 2008
consider the following statistic defined by

\displaystyle T^{\prime}_{SD}=\frac{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})^{T}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})-\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2}-2)p}{n_{1}+n_{2}-4}}{\sqrt{2\big[\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-\frac{p^{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\big]}}.

(4)

Under certain conditions, they show
T^{\prime}_{SD}
converges to a normal distribution. In fact, to improve the convergence speeds, they actually add a term
c_{p,n}=1+\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})}{p^{3/2}}
under the squared roots in the denominators of
T_{SD}
and
T_{SD}^{\prime}
, respectively. Based on their assumptions,
c_{p,n}
goes to one. However, we will study a more general case in which
c_{p,n}
may not go to one, and sometimes it even goes to infinity. This is the reason we dump the term
c_{p,n}
from both
T_{SD}
and
T_{SD}^{\prime}
, respectively.

Evidently,
T_{SD}
and
T^{\prime}_{SD}
have a nice property of scale-invariance, i.e., they are not changed if data are multiplied by a constant. Also, they can be directly computed. Our motivation in this paper to reexamine the tests by
Srivastava and Du 2008
has three folds. (a) The conditions to guarantee the central limit theorems of
T_{SD}
and
T^{\prime}_{SD}
are somehow stringent; see (
8
). Also the sample size and population dimension have to satisfy that
p=o(n^{2})
. To make the method more applicable, we hope to relax the condition imposed on
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
as well as that
p
on
n
. (b) We would like to understand an interesting observation by
Zhang et al. 2020b
from their simulation: the distribution of
T^{\prime}_{SD}
sometimes looks like a normal curve, and other times it looks like a chi-square curve. (c) We plan to give a rigorous proof of the major ingredient of this theory, that is, an asymptotic ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, where
\mathbb{R}
is the population correlation matrix of
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
aforementioned.

Now we state our findings. For (a), we have obtained the asymptotic distributions of
T_{SD}
and
T^{\prime}_{SD}
for arbitrary
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
in Theorems
1
and
2
, respectively. In particular, Theorem
1
holds for two extreme cases: the independent case with
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{I}_{p}
and the most dependent case, i.e., all entries of the population vector are identical. Our theory says the restriction on
p
and
n
will be
p=o(n^{2})
if the entries of the population vector are not far from independent. However, for dependent or very dependent case, our conclusion holds as long as
p
is not more than a polynomial order of
n
. For (b), we successfully understand the observation by
Zhang et al. 2020b
. In fact, there are indeed transition phenomena of the limiting distributions of
T_{SD}
and
T^{\prime}_{SD}
. They are sometimes normal, mixing chi-squared or the sum of two independent random variables, one has normal distribution and the other has a mixing chi-squared distribution. This can be quickly seen from (
11
) and (
17
). As for (c), for arbitrary
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
we have proved rigorously that an asymptotic ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})-\frac{p(p-1)}{n-1}
in Theorem
3
. Especially the theorem is true for two extreme cases:
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is diagonal or proportional to a matrix whose entries are all equal to
1
. In addition, some of our partial calculations and heuristics indicate that the above phase transition from the Gaussian to mixing chi-squared distributions is possibly a universal phenomenon. We will present three testing procedures to justify this claim.

The above solution is conducted through the understanding of the sample correlation matrix
\hat{{\bf R}}
, a special random matrix, defined below (
1
). Unlike the Gaussian orhtogonal/unitary/symplectic ensemble or Wishart matrices,
\hat{{\bf R}}
lacks the orthogonal-invariant property. As a consequence, investigating the sample correlation matrix always cost extra energy than working on other popular matrices. We employ the machinery for
\hat{{\bf R}}
developed by
Jiang 2004
,
Jiang 2009
,
Cai et al. 2013
,
Jiang and Yang 2013
, and
Fan and Jiang 2019
. In particular, we extend the method conceived by
Jiang 2004
and
Fan and Jiang 2019
to prove a weak law of large numbers for
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
en route to obtain an asymptotic ratio-unbiased-estimator. More elaboration are provided in Section
4
.

The remaining of the paper is organized as follows. The one-sample mean and two-sample mean problems are studied in Sections
2
and
3
, respectively. In Section
4
, we give an asymptotic ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, which is more applicable than the one by
Srivastava and Du 2008
and
Srivastava 2009
(their result lacks a rigorous proof although it has been used in literature). The concluding remarks and discussions are presented in Section
5
, in which we particularly point out our findings on phase transitions between normal and mixing chi-squared distributions may also exist for some other testing procedures. Finally, the proofs are given in Section
6
.

2
One Sample Mean Test for Large
p
and Small
n

Let
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}\}
be a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
. Consider the test that

\displaystyle H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}~~\textrm{versus}~~H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\not=\boldsymbol{0}.

(5)

Let
\mathbb{D}
be the diagonal matrix of
\mathbb{\Sigma}.
Then the
p\times p
population correlation matrix is

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{D}^{-1/2}\mathbb{\Sigma}\mathbb{D}^{-1/2}.

(6)

Similarly, let
\hat{{\bf D}}
be the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
from (
1
). Then the sample correlation matrix
\hat{{\bf R}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf R}}=\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}\hat{{\bf S}}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}.

(7)

Srivastava and Du 2008
and
Srivastava 2009
obtain a result on
T_{SD}
from (
2
) as follows.

Result 2.1

Assume
n=O(p^{\zeta})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\zeta\leq 1
and

\displaystyle 0<\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{i})}{p}<\infty,~~~~i=1,2,3,4.

(8)

If
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}
then
T_{SD}\to N(0,1)
in distribution as
p\to\infty.

A quick comment is that (
8
) holds automatically for
i=1
since all of the diagonal entries of
\mathbb{R}
are equal to
1
, and hence
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R})=p
. In order to make approximation better, we now revise the statistic
T_{SD}
slightly. Set

\displaystyle T_{p,1}=\frac{n\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{T}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1}\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}-pn(n-3)^{-1}}{\sqrt{2\big|\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-p(p-1)(n-1)^{-1}\big|}}.

(9)

We are doing so because, by Lemma
6.3
, the major contribution of the mean of
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})+p(p-1)(n-1)^{-1}
. Also, by Theorem
3
,
\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-p(p-1)(n-1)^{-1}>0
as
n
and
p
are large enough. On the other hand, with probability one,
\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-p(p-1)(n-1)^{-1}\neq 0
because
\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})
is a continuous function of Gaussian random variables. A discussion between
T_{p,1}
and
T_{SD}
will be elaborated shortly. Review the Frobenius norm, sometimes also called the Euclidean norm,
\|\mathbb{A}\|_{F}:=[\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{A}^{T}\mathbb{A})]^{1/2}
for any matrix
\mathbb{A}
. For mathematical rigor, we assume the sample sizes
n
depends on
p
. According to
Hu and Bai 2016
, the behavior of the statistic
T_{SD}
is not known if
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
has spikes. The following gives a complete characterization of the properties of
T_{SD}
and
T_{p,1}
in terms of the spikes of the correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}.

T
HEOREM
1

Suppose
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
is a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
. Let
\mathbb{R}
be the correlation matrix as in (
6
) with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0
. Assume

(a)
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=\rho_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\geq 1
;

(b)
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n^{a}}=0
for some constant
a>0
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, then

T_{SD}\to b\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1)
in distribution, where
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
and
b=(1-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
. The same conclusion also holds for
T_{p,1}
.

Condition (a) considers the possibility that
\mathbb{R}
may have spikes. If
\rho_{1}=0
, then
\rho_{i}=0
for every
i\geq 1
due to the monotonicity of
\rho_{i}
, and we say there are no spikes in
\mathbb{R}
. In this situation we have
T_{p,1}\to N(0,1)
by Theorem
1
. Using essentially the Fatou lemma, we have checked the given conditions actually imply
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2}\leq 1
; see the proof of Lemma
4.2
. If
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2}=1
, then
T_{p,1}
converges to a mixing chi-square distribution. If
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2}\in(0,1)
, then the asymptotic distribution is a blend of a Gaussian distribution and a mixing chi-squared distribution. Condition (b) characterizes the restriction between the sample size
n
and population dimension
p
.

Observe that (
8
) with
i=3
implies that
\lambda_{1}^{3}\leq\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{3})=O(p)
. Thus,
\lambda_{1}=O(p^{1/3})
. Since all of the diagonal entries of
\mathbb{R}
are identical to
1
then
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\geq p
. This shows that
\rho_{1}=0
where
\rho_{1}
is from Theorem
1
. Also, (
8
) with
i=2
implies that
\|\mathbb{R}\|
and
\sqrt{p}
have the same order, thus
\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}\to 0
as
p\to\infty
. This together with the condition “
n=O(p^{\zeta})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\zeta\leq 1
” implies (b) from Theorem
1
. So our theorem is more general than Result
2.1
by
Srivastava and Du 2008
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from an
AR(1)
model with
\mathbb{R}=(\gamma^{|i-j|})
and the absolute values of
\gamma=\gamma_{p}
staying away from
1
. By using the Gersgorin disc theorem [see, e.g., p. 344 from
Horn and Johnson 2012
], the largest eigenvalue or
\mathbb{R}
is of order
O(1)
. Hence condition (a) of Theorem
1
holds with
\rho_{1}=0
. If condition (b) also holds, then both
T_{SD}
and
T_{p,1}
go to the standard normal distribution. The same conclusion is also valid for a banded correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})
with
r_{ij}=0
for
|j-i|\geq t
where
t=t_{p}=o(\sqrt{p})
. In this case, the largest eigenvalue or
\mathbb{R}
is of order
o(\sqrt{p})
. Similar results can be obtained for other patterned matrices including Toeplitz matrices, Hankel matrices and symmetric circulant matrices; see, e.g.,
Brockwell et al. 2016
.

Now let us look at some special features of Theorem
1
. First, we do not need the population matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
to be invertible as required in Hotelling’s
T^{2}
test
(
Hotelling 1931
)
. The largest discrepancy between
n
and
p
from
Srivastava and Du 2008
is that
p=o(n^{2})
. Our range is that
p
can be at any polynomial order of
n
provided
p=o(n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F})
. If the entries of the population vector are not very far from independence, in the sense that
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
is in the order of
\sqrt{p}
, then the restriction
p=o(n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F})
is reduced to
p=o(n^{2})
. In the case that the entries of the population vector are very dependent such that
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
is in the order of
p
, then
p
is allowed to take any polynomial order of
n
. To convince our readers for the dependent case and to make further discussions, we next study two extreme cases: independence and most dependence. The derivation of the results below does not use any techniques and steps from the proof of Theorem
1
.

P
ROPOSITION
1

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\mathbb{0}
. Assume
n=n_{p}\to\infty
as
p\to\infty
. The following hold.

(i) Let all of the
p^{2}
entries of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
be identical. Then both
T_{SD}
and
T_{p,1}
converge to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\chi^{2}(1)-1]
in distribution as
p\to\infty
regardless of the relative speeds of
n
and
p
.

(ii) Let
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
be a diagonal matrix whose diagonal entries are all positive, equivalently,
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{I}_{p}
. Then

\displaystyle T_{SD}\to\begin{cases}\eta,&\text{if $p/n^{2}\to 0$};\\
\eta+\sqrt{h/2},&\text{if $p/n^{2}\to h$};\\
\infty,&\text{if $p/n^{2}\to\infty$}\end{cases}

(10)

in distribution, where
\eta\sim N(0,1)
. However,
T_{p,1}\to N(0,1)

as
p\to\infty
regardless of the speeds of
n
and
p
going to infinity.

In case (i) we see Theorem
1
holds without the assumption that
p=o(n^{a})
for some constant
a>0
.
The conclusion for case (ii) says that the condition
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
in Theorem
1
is sharp, and
T_{p,1}
is better than
T_{SD}
. In particular, we do not need the assumption
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
to assure
T_{p,1}\to N(0,1)
. This is simply a coincidence because of the special structure of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
or
\mathbb{R}
. The condition
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
is essentially required at handling the denominator of
T_{p,1}
for arbitrary
\mathbb{R}
; see (
9
) and Theorem
3
. Figure
1
clearly shows the existence of shifts between the density curves of
T_{SD}
and
N(0,1)
. However, with the ratio
p/n^{2}
becoming smaller, the shift diminishes gradually.

Figure 1:
These pictures are based on Proposition
1
, from
10^{5}
simulations. It shows the centers of
T_{p,1}
and
N(0,1)
are always close, and the existence of shifts between density curves of
T_{SD}
and
N(0,1)
. The shifts are smaller as
p/n^{2}
become smaller.

Practically, given the population correlation
\mathbb{R}
, we need to determine if it has spikes and how many spikes. Interested readers are referred to
Fan et al. 2020
and
Morales-Jimenez et al. 2021
for procedures to do so. We next present some examples. Their verification are presented in Section
6.4
.

Example 2.1

Given
r\in[0,1]
, define
\mathbb{A}_{m}:=(1-r)\mathbb{I}_{m}+r\mathbb{J}_{m}

for any
m\geq 1
, where
\mathbb{I}_{p}
and
\mathbb{J}_{p}
denote the
p\times p
identity matrix and the
p\times p
matrix of ones.
Set
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{A}_{p}
. Then
\lambda_{1}=1+(p-1)r
and
\lambda_{2}=\cdots=\lambda_{p}=1-r
. Assume
r=r_{p}
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}\sqrt{p}\cdot r=c\geq 0
. Then

\displaystyle T_{p}\to\begin{cases}\xi_{0},&\text{if $c=0$};\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+1}}\xi_{0}+\frac{c}{\sqrt{2(c^{2}+1)}}(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $c\in(0,\infty)$};\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $c=\infty$}\end{cases}

(11)

under condition
p=o(n^{2})
for the case
c\in[0,\infty)
and under the conditions
nr\to\infty
and
p=o(n^{a})
for some constant
a>0
for the case
c=\infty
. Here
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
and
T_{p}
stands for
T_{SD}
or
T_{p,1}
. The phase transition occurs between the normal and mixing chi-squared distributions as the entries of the correlation matrix change their values.

We make Figures
2
and
3
based on Example
2.1
to compare the performances of our
T_{p,1}
and
T_{SD}
by
Srivastava and Du 2008
. For different values of
n,p,r
, the statistics
T_{p,1}
and
T_{SD}
are simulated for
10^{5}
times. The elaboration is given below.

Figure
2
is designed for a small value of
r
with
r=0.1
. Look at the first two pictures on the top row, that is, the ones with
(n,p)=(10,50)
and
(n,p)=(20,100)
, respectively. Neither
T_{p,1}
nor
T_{SD}
perform well. But this is expected because the sample sizes are very small. However, by fixing the rate of
p/n
and let
n
and
p
grow gradually, the approximations become better and better. In particular our theoretical curves are always close to the empirical ones, whereas the normal ones stay farther or much farther from the empirical ones.

Figure
3
is designed for a big value of
r
with
r=0.5
. Except the first two pictures on the top row, which correspond to
(n,p)=(10,50)
and
(n,p)=(20,100)
, respectively, our theoretical curves match the empirical ones well. As the explanation aforementioned, that the two simulations do not behave well is understandable, simply because the sample sizes are very small. Our theoretical curves are constantly close to the empirical ones, the normal ones are nowhere close to the empirical ones.

In summary, the simulation indicates our approximation stated in Theorem
1
that
(1-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2})^{1/2}\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1)
to
T_{SD}
and
T_{p,1}
, respectively, outperforms the normal approximation from
Result
2.1
.

Figure 2:
Example
2.1
. For small
r=0.1
, the empirical curves in blue are close to our theoretical curves in red. As sample size
n
becomes larger, our approximation becomes better; the normal approximation (black curve) is no longer valid.

Figure 3:
Example
2.1
. Compared to Figure
2
with
r=0.1
, we take
r=0.5
here. The empirical curves in blue are close to our theoretical curves in red. The normal approximation (black curve) from
Result
2.1
is no longer valid.

Example 2.2

For
r\in(0,1)
, set
m=[p^{r}]
. Recall
\mathbb{A}_{m}
from Example
2.1
. Define
\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{A}_{m}&\mathbb{0}\\
\mathbb{0}&\mathbb{I}_{p-m}\end{pmatrix}_{p\times p},

where the two “
r
” from “
m=[p^{r}]
” and “
\mathbb{A}_{m}
” are the same one.
Assuming
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{p}\to\begin{cases}\xi_{0}&\text{if $0<r<\frac{1}{2}$};\\
\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{10}}\cdot(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $r=\frac{1}{2}$};\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $\frac{1}{2}<r<1$}\end{cases}

(12)

under condition
p=o(n^{2})
for
0<r\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
p=o(n^{1/(1-r)})
for
\frac{1}{2}<r<1
, where
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
as
r=\frac{1}{2}
. Here
T_{p}
stands for
T_{SD}
or
T_{p,1}
. Obviously, there is a phase transition at
r=\frac{1}{2}
as
r
runs between
0
and
1
. The phase transition appears at both relative sizes of
n
and
p
together with the change of the values of matrix entries.

Example 2.3

Set
m=[\log(p+2)].
Given
\tau\geq 0
, define integer
p^{\prime}=p-[\tau\sqrt{p}]
,
\lambda_{i}=1+\tau 2^{-i}\big(1-2^{-m}\big)^{-1}\sqrt{p}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
\lambda_{i}=1
for
i=m+1,\cdots,p^{\prime}-1
,
\lambda_{p^{\prime}}=1+[\tau\sqrt{p}]-\tau\sqrt{p}\in[0,1]
, and the rest of
\lambda_{i}
are identical to
0
.
By Lemma
4.3
, there exists a correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathbb{R}
has eigenvalues
\lambda_{i},\,1\leq i\leq p.
Assuming
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, we have

\displaystyle T_{p}\to\begin{cases}\xi_{0},&\text{if $\tau=0$};\\
\sqrt{\frac{3}{\tau^{2}+3}}\xi_{0}+\sqrt{\frac{3\tau^{2}}{2(\tau^{2}+3)}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{i}}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1),&\text{if $\tau\in(0,\infty)$};\\
\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{i}}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1),&\text{if $\tau\to\infty$}\end{cases}

(13)

in distribution as
p\to\infty
under condition
p=o(n^{2})
. Here
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{d}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1).
Here
T_{p}
stands for
T_{SD}
or
T_{p,1}
. The statistic
T_{p}
behaves like a rainbow, which has a Gaussian distribution at one end, and a mixing chi-squared distribution at the horizon, and a hybrid in between.

An interesting remark is that, unlike the eigenvalues of a covariance matrix, the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0
of the
p\times p
correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
cannot be arbitrary. By Lemma
4.3
, they have to satisfy the so-called “majorization” property:
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}\geq k
for every
1\leq k\leq p
and
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{p}=p
. Conversely, for any
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0
with the “majorization” property, there is always a correlation matrix with eigenvalues
\lambda_{i},\,1\leq i\leq p
.

3
Two Sample Mean Test for Large
p
and Small
n

We will study the two-sample mean testing problem
in the high-dimensional setting in this section. As before, assume that

\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{in_{i}}\}
for
i=1,2
are two independent
random samples with sizes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
, and from
p
-variate
normal distributions
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, respectively. We
wish to test

\displaystyle H_{0}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}\ \ \mbox{versus}\ \ H_{1}:{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}\neq{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}.

(14)

Let
\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{i}
be the sample mean of the
i
-th sample with
i=1,2
and
\hat{{\bf S}}
be the pooled sample covariance matrix defined in (
3
). Set
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
.
Srivastava and Du 2008
consider the statistic
T^{\prime}_{SD}
from (
4
).
Recall
\hat{{\bf D}}
is the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
in (
3
) and

\displaystyle\hat{{\bf R}}=\hat{{\bf R}}_{p}=\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}\hat{{\bf S}}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}

(15)

is the pooled sample correlation matrix.
Similar to the discussion before (
9
) on the modification of
T_{SD}
, we make a little change for
T_{SD}^{\prime}
as follows. Define

\displaystyle T_{p,2}=\frac{\frac{n_{1}n_{2}}{n_{1}+n_{2}-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})^{T}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})-\frac{(n_{1}+n_{2}-1)p}{n_{1}+n_{2}-4}}{\sqrt{2\big|\mathrm{tr}(\hat{{\bf R}}^{2})-\frac{p(p-1)}{n_{1}+n_{2}-2}\big|}}.

(16)

Srivastava and Du 2008
derive the limiting distribution of
T_{SD}^{\prime}
under conditions similar to those from
Result
2.1
and an extra assumption “
\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\to c\in(0,1)
”. On the other hand,
Hu and Bai 2016
ask for the properties of
T_{SD}^{\prime}
as the variances of the entries of the population vector are quite different. We will provide a solution for
T^{\prime}_{SD}
and
T_{p,2}
next. For mathematical rigor, we assume that both sample sizes
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
depend on
p
.

T
HEOREM
2

Let
\{{\boldsymbol{X}}_{i1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{in_{i}}\}
for
i=1,2
be two independent random samples from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
, respectively. Let
\mathbb{R}
be as in (
6
) with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0
. Assume

(a)
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=\rho_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\geq 1
;

(b)
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{(n_{1}+n_{2})\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{(n_{1}+n_{2})^{a}}=0
for some constant
a>0
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{1}={\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{2}
, then

T_{p,2}\to b\xi_{0}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1)

in distribution, where
\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
and
b=(1-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
. The same conclusion also holds for “
T_{p,2}
”.

The above result does not require condition “
\min\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\to\infty
” or “
\frac{n_{1}}{n_{1}+n_{2}}\to\gamma\in(0,1)
”, which are commonly assumed in literature for the two-sample testing problem.

As discussed below Theorem
1
, condition
(
8
) implies that
\rho_{1}=0
and
\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}\to 0
as
p\to\infty
. Condition “
n_{1}+n_{2}=O(p^{\zeta})
,
\frac{1}{2}<\zeta\leq 1
” from (
8
) implies (b) from Theorem
2
. So our theorem is more general than the conclusion for the two-sample test by
Srivastava and Du 2008
.

If
\mathbb{R}
is
AR(1)
model, the banded model, the Toeplitz or Hankel matrices, please see the discussion below Theorem
1
. They are also applied here.

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from an
AR(1)
model with
\mathbb{R}=(\gamma^{|i-j|})
and the absolute values of
\gamma=\gamma_{p}
staying away from
1
. By using the Gersgorin disc theorem [see, e.g., p. 344 from
Horn and Johnson 2012
], the largest eigenvalue or
\mathbb{R}
is of order
O(1)
. Hence condition (a) of Theorem
1
holds with
\rho_{1}=0
. If condition (b) also holds, then both
T_{SD}
and
T_{p,1}
go to the standard normal distribution. The same conclusion is also valid for a banded correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})
with
r_{ij}=0
for
|j-i|\geq t
where
t=t_{p}=o(\sqrt{p})
. In this case, the largest eigenvalue or
\mathbb{R}
is of order
o(\sqrt{p})
. Similar results can be obtained for other patterned matrices including Toeplitz matrices, Hankel matrices and symmetric circulant matrices; see, e.g.,
Brockwell et al. 2016
.

Recall the definition of
T_{p,2}
from (
16
). Let
\mathbb{D}
be the diagonal matrix of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. Under some conditions including
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
is of the order
p
,
Zhang et al. 2020b
obtain the limiting distribution of a normalized
(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})^{T}{\bf D}^{-1}(\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{1}-\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}_{2})
as
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}(\xi_{i}^{2}-1)
. Although this quantity is not directly applicable because the unknown parameter matrix
{\bf D}
is involved, it is indeed suggestive. Their result seems to be a special case of Theorem
2
with
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2}=1
. In fact, under their assumptions, we have confirmed that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2}=1
in Lemma

6.37
from Section
6.4
.
Notice
\sqrt{p}\leq\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}\leq p
for any
\mathbb{R}
.
Srivastava and Du 2008
consider the extreme case that
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
is of the scale of
\sqrt{p}
. This is essentially a weakly dependent situation with
\rho_{i}=0
for each
i\geq 1
. According to Theorem
2
, the limiting distribution is a normal. On the other hand, roughly speaking,
Zhang et al. 2020b
intend to study another extreme case in which
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
is of the scale
p
, and hence
\mathbb{R}
is completely a spiked model. As explained above, the corresponding limiting distribution is a mixing chi-squared distribution. Our Theorem
2
handles the case for any
\mathbb{R}
with
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}
running everywhere between
\sqrt{p}
and
p
, and the limiting distribution turns out to be, interestingly enough, a convolution of both.

Now we demonstrate our results by giving some examples.

Example 3.1

Recall Example
2.1
and

\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{A}_{p}
. For this example,
Zhang et al. 2020b
observe that “When
r=0.01
, the histograms are quite symmetric and bell-shaped, indicating that a normal approximation as suggested by the theory of
Srivastava and Du 2008
may be applied for approximating the null distribution of
T_{SD}
. However, when
r=0.5
and
r=0.9
, the histograms are quite skewed, indicating that a normal approximation is no longer adequate.” In fact, our Theorem
2
explains their inspection very accurately as follows. Assume
r=r_{p}
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}\sqrt{p}\cdot r=c\geq 0
. By changing “
n
” in Example
2.1
to “
n_{1}+n_{2}
”, we have

\displaystyle T_{p}\to\begin{cases}\xi_{0},&\text{if $c=0$};\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{c^{2}+1}}\xi_{0}+\frac{c}{\sqrt{2(c^{2}+1)}}(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $c\in(0,\infty)$};\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot(\xi_{1}^{2}-1),&\text{if $c=\infty$}\end{cases}

(17)

under condition
p=o((n_{1}+n_{2})^{2})
for the case
c\in[0,\infty)
and under the condition
p=o((n_{1}+n_{2})^{a})
for some constant
a>0
for the case
c=\infty
, where
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1).
Here
T_{p}
stands for
T_{SD}^{\prime}
or
T_{p,2}
. Obviously, there is a phase transition between
c=0
and
c=\infty
.

Figure 4:
Example
3.1
. Our theoretical curves always match the empirical ones well no matter the former is a normal-like or chi-square-like curve, even one of
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
being small. The normal curves by
Srivastava and Du 2008
are farther from the empirical ones as correlation
r
increases.

Similar to Example
3.1
, modifications of Examples
2.2
and
2.3
can also be constructed for the two-sample case. It is straightforward, so we omit the detail. Given a data set, the spikes of the population correlation
\mathbb{R}
have to be determined; see
Fan et al. 2020
and
Morales-Jimenez et al. 2021
.

Based on Example
3.1
, the statistic
T_{p,2}
from (
16
) is simulated for
10^{5}
times for each set of values of
(n_{1},n_{2},p,r)
. The value of
r
is chosen at
0,1,0,5
and
0.9
, designed for weakly dependence, dependence and strong dependence, respectively. Our theoretical curves are normal-like, mixing between
N(0,1)
and chi-squared distribution and chi-squared-like curves. For weakly dependent case, by examining the four graphs in the first column, we see the normal approximation by
Srivastava and Du 2008
becomes less and less accurate as all of the values of
n_{1},n_{2},p
increase. However, our theoretical curve always match the empirical ones well. For dependent and very dependent cases appeared in the second and third columns, respectively, the normal curves are nowhere close to the empirical ones. It is good to see our theoretical curves are always close to their empirical counterparts.

4
A Law of Large Numbers for Sample Correlation Matrices

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{R}}
be the sample correlation matrix defined in (
7
). One of the crucial steps in proving Theorems
1
and
2
is the use of an asymptotically ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. In their Lemma 3.2,
Srivastava and Du 2008
state the following.

Result 4.1

If
n=O(p^{\zeta})
,
0<\zeta\leq 1
, under the condition (
8
),

(\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})-p^{2}/n)/p
converges to
\lim_{p\to\infty}\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})/p
in probability as
(n,p)\to\infty
and thus can be considered as a consistent estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})/p
as
(n,p)\to\infty
.

The proof of the above result is give on pages 400-402 from
Srivastava and Du 2008
. It lacks a mathematical rigor.
By using a method from the random matrix theory, we rigorously obtain a more general result than the above in the following. Most efforts in the whole proofs of this paper are devoted to this result, which is rather involved. The reason is that we assume no independence at all among the entries of the population distribution, i.e.,
\mathbb{R}
is arbitrary.

T
HEOREM
3

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{R}}
be the sample correlation matrix defined in (
7
). If
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
and
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n^{a}}=0
for some constant
a>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\Big[\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})-\frac{p(p-1)}{n-1}\Big]\to 1

in probability as
p\to\infty.

Unlike
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
needed in
Result
4.1
, our conditions in the above theorem are imposed on
\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}=[\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})]^{1/2}
only. Also, it is easy to see the difference between the assumptions on restrictions of
n
and
p
:
Result
4.1
asks
n=O(p^{\zeta})
for some
0<\zeta\leq 1
and our Theorem
3
holds as long as
n
and
p
are in the order of a polynomial. We make some remarks on Theorem
3
next.

Naively, an obvious ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is its sample version
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Theorem
3
indicates that it is not true for the high dimensional setting. Instead, the modified version “
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})-\frac{p(p-1)}{n-1}
” is a correct one. By using the fact
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})\geq p
, we can see from Theorem
3
that
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
is indeed a ratio-unbiased-estimator of
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
if
p
is as small as
p=o(n)
, that is,
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})/\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to 1
in probability.

Sample correlation matrices are a special type of random matrices. The spectral distributions of eigenvalue values are studied in
Jiang 2004
and
Xiao and Zhou 2010
. The central limit theorems for determinants under independent and correlated situations are obtained by
Jiang and Yang 2013
and
Jiang 2009
, respectively. The Tracy-Widom law is derived by
Bao et al. 2012
. The central limit theorem for linear statistics of eigenvalues is understood by
Gao et al. 2017
.

An interesting fact is the derivation of Theorem
3
. Through the whole proof, contrary to standard techniques to handle randomness, we do not use/assume any independence from the population distribution
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
. In fact, in the most dependent case, that is, all of the
p
entries of
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
are identical, then
\hat{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}
and all of their
p^{2}
entries are equal to
1
, so
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})=\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=p^{2}
. One can see Theorem
3
trivially holds. On the other hand, for the most independent case, i.e., if
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{I}_{p}
, then
\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=p.
Assuming
p/n\to\rho\in(0,1]
, we have from Theorem
3
that
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})/p\to 1+\rho.
This actually can be confirmed independently by a random matrix theory. In fact, let
\mu_{p}
be the empirical distribution of the eigenvalues of
\hat{\mathbb{R}}
. By Theorem 2 from
Jiang 2004
,
\mu_{p}
converges weakly to the so-called Marčhenko-Pastur law
\mu
. The second moment of
\mu
is equal to
1+\rho
[Lemma 3.1 from
Bai and Silverstein 2010
]. Consequently,
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})/p=\int x^{2}\,d\mu_{p}\to\int x^{2}\,d\mu=1+\rho.

The proof of Theorem
3
is a bit technical and lengthy. We first give an accurate estimate of
E\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
in Lemma
6.3
. Then we only need to show that its variance go to zero. To do so, since
\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2}=p+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}\hat{r}_{ij}^{2}
, we need to understand the covariance between two sample correlations, say,
\hat{r}_{ij}^{2}
and
\hat{r}_{kl}^{2}
for any
1\leq i,j,k,l\leq p
. The precise result is given next.

T
HEOREM
4

Assume
m\geq 5
. Let
\{(X_{1j},X_{2j},X_{3j},X_{4j})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{4};\,1\leq j\leq m\}
be i.i.d. random vectors with distribution
N_{4}(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})_{4\times 4}
and
r_{ii}=1
for each
i
. Set

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{ij}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{ik}X_{jk}}{(\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{ik}^{2})^{1/2}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{jk}^{2})^{1/2}}

(18)

for
1\leq i\leq j\leq 4.
Then, for any
N\geq 1
,
\mbox{Cov}(\hat{r}_{12}^{2},\hat{r}_{34}^{2})
is equal to

\displaystyle\varrho_{m,1}\cdot\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}r_{ij}^{2}+\varrho_{m,2}\cdot r_{12}^{2}r_{34}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varrho_{m,3}\cdot\big(r_{12}r_{23}r_{34}r_{41}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{43}r_{31}\big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varrho_{m,4}\cdot\big(r_{13}r_{34}r_{41}+r_{23}r_{34}r_{42}\big)r_{12}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\varrho_{m,5}\cdot\big(r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{41}\big)r_{34}^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{m,6}}{m^{(N+1)/2}},

where
\{\varrho_{m,i};\,3\leq i\leq 5\}
are quantities not depending on
\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle|\varrho_{m,1}|\leq Km^{-2},\ \ |\varrho_{m,2}|\vee|\varrho_{m,3}|\vee|\varrho_{m,4}|\vee|\varrho_{m,5}|\leq Km^{-1},\ |\varrho_{m,6}|\leq K

and
K
is a constant depending on
N
but not on
m
or
\mathbb{R}.

The proof of Theorem
4
is rather involved. There are two reasons, one of them is that no assumption is imposed on
\mathbb{R}
, as a consequence, we are not able to use any techniques/theory related to independence. The second one is that sample correlation coefficients are more difficult to be handled than sample covariances. Look at (
18
) for
\hat{r}_{12}
by taking
i=1
and
j=2
. From the law of large numbers,
(1/m)\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{1k}^{2}\to 1
and
(1/m)\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{2k}^{2}\to 1
. To understand
\hat{r}_{12}
, a naive idea is to replace the denominator in the expression of
\hat{r}_{12}
by
m
. Interestingly, this works for the derivation of the Marčhenko-Pastur law (Jiang, 2004) when
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{I}
. Now we elaborate this more for a further discussion. By the Taylor expansion, write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+x}}=1-\frac{x}{2}+\frac{3}{8}x^{2}+\cdots+a_{N}x^{N}+o(x^{N+1})

(19)

as
x
is small.
Jiang 2004
uses the above expansion with
N=0
and
x
taking
(1/m)\sum_{k=1}^{m}(X_{1k}^{2}-1)
and
(1/m)\sum_{k=1}^{m}(X_{2k}^{2}-1)
, respectively. If
\mathbb{R}\neq\mathbb{I}
but
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{A}_{p}
as in Example
2.1
,
Fan and Jiang 2019
use the expansion (
19
) with
N=1
. In the proof our Theorem
3
, since no structure of
\mathbb{R}
is assumed, we have to use (
19
) for an arbitrary
N
. This is why the condition “
\limsup_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n^{a}}=0
for some constant
a>0
” is imposed in Theorems
1
and
3
. The new method here would be useful in the future for the study on sample correlation matrices. It is worthwhile to mention that handling sample correlation matrices is much more difficult than working on sample covariance matrices. The difference is obvious: the entries of a sample covariance matrix does not have the denominator as in the expression of
\hat{r}_{ij}
from (
18
). For instance,
Bai and Saranadasa 1996
also investigate a ratio-unbiased-estimator similar to Theorem
3
but for covariance matrices, their argument is quick and short.

A byproduct of the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
is the behavior of a quadratic form related to the diagonal matrix of
\mathbb{R}
. It would be useful for other research from this point forward. Assume
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
is a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
. Review
{\bf D}
is the diagonal matrix of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
and
\hat{{\bf D}}
is the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
in (
3
).

L
EMMA
4.1

Let
\boldsymbol{\eta}\sim N_{p}(\mathbb{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
and
\boldsymbol{\eta}
be independent of
\hat{{\bf S}}
. If
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{T}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\sqrt{2\,\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}=\frac{\boldsymbol{\eta}^{T}{\bf D}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\eta}}{\sqrt{2\,\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}}+o_{p}(1)

as
p\to\infty.

Since
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
is a random sample from a multivariate normal distribution,
\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}
is independent of
\hat{{\bf S}}
, and hence
\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}
is independent of
\hat{{\bf D}}
. So the above conclusion holds if we take
\boldsymbol{\eta}=\sqrt{p}\bar{{\boldsymbol{X}}}
. This is actually the way we use this lemma in later proofs.

The limiting distributions in Theorems
1
and
2
essentially come from the following.

L
EMMA
4.2

For each
p\geq 1
, let
a_{p,1}\geq\cdots\geq a_{p,p}\geq 0

be constants with
a_{p,1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{p,p}^{2}=1
. Suppose
\lim_{p\to\infty}a_{p,i}=\rho_{i}\geq 0
for each
i\geq 1
. Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots
be i.i.d. with distribution
\chi^{2}(1)-1.
Then
a_{p,1}\xi_{1}+\cdots+a_{p,p}\xi_{p}
converges to
[2(1-\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}^{2})]^{1/2}\eta+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{i}\xi_{i}
in distribution as
p\to\infty
, where
\eta\sim N(0,1)
and
\eta
is independent of
\{\xi_{i};\,i\geq 1\}
.

The comment after Example
2.3
is based on the following fact. It is interesting useful in its own right.

L
EMMA
4.3

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a
p\times p
correlation matrix, namely,
\mathbb{M}
is non-negative definite and its diagonal entries are all equal to
1
. Suppose
\mathbb{M}
has eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}
. Then
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}\geq k
for
k=1,\cdots,p
and
\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{p}=p
. Conversely, for any
\tau_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\tau_{p}\geq 0
with
\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{k}\geq k
for each
k=1,\cdots,p
and
\tau_{1}+\cdots+\tau_{p}=p
, there always exists a correlation matrix with eigenvalues
\tau_{1},\cdots,\tau_{p}
.

5
Concluding Remarks and Discussion

1. In this paper we have studied one-sample and two-sample mean tests. For the multiple population case, it becomes the classical MANOVA problem. In the “large
p
, small
n
” situation,
Srivastava and Fujikoshi 2006
consider the question by revising the classical
F
-test. They use functions of non-zero eigenvalues of pseudo-MANOVA random matrix as test statistics.
Zhang et al. 2017
and
Chen et al. 2019
study this case based on the
L_{2}
-type statistics. An extension of our work to the MANOVA case under “large
p
, small
n
” situation is meaningful. One direction is to modify the classical tests, such as the Anderson test, the Pillal test, the Roy test, the Wilks test and the Olson test, by a method similar to those from
Srivastava and Fujikoshi 2006
. The classical statistics take the correlations among population coordinates into account. It has kind of self-normalization, which is favorable.

2. All results in this paper are based on random samples from a multivariate Gaussian distribution. Is it possible to generalize this to non-Gaussian cases? The corresponding results are highly demanded simply because many data are not sampled from a Gaussian distribution. Also, conclusions on non-Gaussian scenario may help us understand robustness of our tests.

3. We study the two-sample mean test by assuming the two populations have the same covariance matrices. If the two covariance matrices are not identical, this is the multivariate Behrens-Fisher problem. Various methods are proposed, for example, by
Yao 1965
,
Johansen 1980
,
Nel and Van der Merwe 1986
,
Krishnamoorthy and Yu 2004
,
Chen and Qin 2010
and
Chen et al. 2019
. For one-dimensional case (
p=1
), the method initiated by
Welch 1947
is probably the most popular one. The author basically normalize the difference of sample means by its sample standard deviation.
Srivastava et al. 2013
propose a statistic by replacing
\hat{\mathbb{D}}
from (
16
) with a hybrid of two samples. Similar to the study in this paper, its properties are needed to be understood, too.

4. As far as proofs go, we spend most of our energy proving Theorem
4
. We do not use complicated technology. Instead, by we use brute force to compute mixed moments of multiple Gaussian random variables in combination with some random matrix theory. As a result, the argument is lengthy. It is possible to shorten the proof. To get the law of large numbers stated in Theorem
4
, one may like to try Gaussian concentration inequalities; see, for example,
Ledoux 2001
and
Boucheron et al. 2013
. An alternative way is to get the joint density function of
\hat{r}_{12}
and
\hat{r}_{34}
similar to that of the marginal density of
\hat{r}_{12}
treated at (
26
) through an hypergeometric function.

5. Theorems
1
and
2
present the null distributions for the one-sample and two-sample tests, respectively. We actually have tried to derive the non-null limiting distribution to explore power functions of the tests. The argument is also very involved. The current paper is already very lengthy, so we postpone and leave it as a future work.

6
Proofs

One of the main steps of proving Theorems
1
and
2
is a weak law of large numbers for
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
(Theorem
3
), where
\hat{\mathbb{R}}=\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
is the sample correlation matrix defined in (
7
). To derive this, we need to study the mean and variance of
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. In Section
6.1
, we will get an accurate estimate of
E[\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})]
by an argument on hypergeometric functions. Then we will show
\mbox{Var}(\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2}))
go to zero. The proof of Theorem
3
will be completed once this step is established. This is a rather involved step and it will be understood step by step in Sections
6.2.1
-
6.2.6
.

6.1
Evaluation of the Mean of the Frobenius Norm of a Sample Correlation matrix

In this section, we will work on the mean of
\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, where
\hat{\mathbb{R}}=\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
is the sample correlation matrix defined in (
7
). The critical tool is the hypergeometric function. We first need a preliminary result as follows.

L
EMMA
6.1

There exists a constant
C>0
such that the following holds for all
k\geq 2
.

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\binom{n+k}{k}^{-1}\leq Ck^{-1/4}.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
. For given
a>0
, set
f_{a}(x)=(1+ax^{-1})^{-x}
for
x>0
. We claim that
f_{a}(x)
is decreasing in
x\in(0,\infty).
In fact, let
g(x)=\log f_{a}(x)
. Then

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)=\Big[-x\log\Big(1+\frac{a}{x}\Big)\Big]^{\prime}=-\log\Big(1+\frac{a}{x}\Big)-x\frac{-\frac{a}{x^{2}}}{1+\frac{a}{x}}.

Then

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)=\log\Big(1-\frac{a}{x+a}\Big)+\frac{a}{x+a}\leq 0

for all
x>0
since
\log(1+y)\leq y
for all
y>-1
. Hence,
f_{a}(x)
is decreasing in
x\in(0,\infty)
for each
a>0.
By the Stirling formula,
m!=\sqrt{2\pi m}m^{m}e^{-m+\frac{\theta_{m}}{12m}}
with
\theta_{m}\in(0,1)
for each
m\geq 1
. Consequently,

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\pi m}m^{m}e^{-m}\leq m!\leq e\cdot\sqrt{2\pi m}m^{m}e^{-m}

for all
m\geq 1.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\binom{n+k}{k}=\frac{(n+k)!}{n!k!}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-2}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n+k}}{\sqrt{2\pi nk}}\cdot\frac{(n+k)^{n+k}e^{-(n+k)}}{k^{k}n^{n}e^{-(n+k)}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle e^{-2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi k}}\cdot\frac{(n+k)^{n+k}}{k^{k}n^{n}}.

This implies that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\binom{n+k}{k}}\leq(2\pi e^{2}k^{1/2})\Big(1+\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{n}{k}\Big)^{-k}.

(20)

For fixed
k
, since
f_{k}(x)
is decreasing in
x
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{n}{k}\Big)^{-k}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{-n}

(21)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k+1}+\sum_{n=2}^{k}\int_{n-1}^{n}\Big(1+\frac{k}{x}\Big)^{-x}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k+1}+\int_{1}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{x}\Big)^{-x}\,dx.

Again, use the fact that
f_{k}(x)
is decreasing to see

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{x}\Big)^{-x}\,dx

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{k^{1/4}}\Big(1+\frac{k}{x}\Big)^{-x}\,dx+\int_{k^{1/4}}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{x}\Big)^{-x}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{k^{1/4}}{k+1}+k\cdot(1+k^{3/4})^{-k^{1/4}}.

If
k\geq 81
, then
k^{1/4}\geq 3
. Consequently,
k\cdot(1+k^{3/4})^{-k^{1/4}}\leq k\cdot(k^{3/4})^{-3}=k^{-5/4}.
From (
21
) we see

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k}\Big(1+\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{n}{k}\Big)^{-k}\leq\frac{1}{k+1}+\frac{k^{1/4}}{k+1}+\frac{1}{k^{5/4}}\leq\frac{3}{k^{3/4}}.

(22)

Now,
(1+\frac{k}{n})^{-n}\leq 2^{-k}
as
n\geq k
by the fact
f_{k}(x)
is decreasing. Also,
(1+\frac{n}{k})^{-k}\leq(1+\frac{n}{k})^{-81}
as
k\geq 81
. Hence

\displaystyle\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\Big(1+\frac{k}{n}\Big)^{-n}\Big(1+\frac{n}{k}\Big)^{-k}\leq 2^{-k}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\Big(1+\frac{n}{k}\Big)^{-81}\leq 2^{-k}k^{81}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{81}}

(23)

as
k\geq 81.
This joined with (
20
), (
22
) and (
23
) yields that

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{k^{1/4}}{\binom{n+k}{k}}\leq k^{1/4}\cdot(2\pi e^{2}k^{1/2})\cdot\big(3k^{-3/4}+\zeta(81)2^{-k}k^{81}\big)\leq C^{\prime}

for all
k\geq 81
, where
\zeta(81)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{81}}<\infty
is the Riemann zeta function evaluated at
81
and
C^{\prime}
is another numerical constant not depending on
k
. In summary,

\displaystyle\sup_{k\geq 81}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}k^{1/4}\binom{n+k}{k}^{-1}<\infty.

(24)

Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{n+i+1}{i+1}}{\binom{n+i}{i}}=\frac{n+i+1}{i+1}>1

for any
i\geq 1
and
n\geq 1
. We know
\binom{n+i}{i}
is increasing in
i
, and hence

\displaystyle\sup_{2\leq k\leq 80}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}k^{1/4}\binom{n+k}{k}^{-1}\leq 80^{1/4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\binom{n+2}{2}^{-1}\leq 2\cdot 80^{1/4}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}<\infty.

This and (
24
) imply the conclusion.
\square

The following result quantifies the second moment of the sample correlation coefficient of a random sample of size
m
up to an error
O(m^{-1/4}).

L
EMMA
6.2

Let
\{(X_{i},Y_{i})^{T};\,1\leq i\leq m\}
be i.i.d.
2
-dimensional normal random vectors with
EX_{1}=EY_{1}=0
,
EX_{1}^{2}=EY_{1}^{2}=1
and
\mbox{Cov}(X_{1},Y_{1})=r
. Set

\displaystyle\hat{r}_{m}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{i}Y_{i}}{(\sum_{k=1}^{m}X_{i}^{2})^{1/2}(\sum_{k=1}^{m}Y_{i}^{2})^{1/2}}.

Write

\displaystyle E\big(\hat{r}_{m}^{2})=\frac{1}{m}+r^{2}+b_{m}(r)\cdot r^{2}

for
m\geq 4
. Then
\sup_{m\geq 4,\,|r|\leq 1}|m^{1/4}b_{m}(r)|<\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
6.2
. If
r=\pm 1
, since
EX_{1}=EY_{1}=0
and
EX_{1}^{2}=EY_{1}^{2}=1
, we know
Y_{i}=\pm X_{i}
for each
i
. By the definition of
\hat{r}_{m}
, trivially,
\hat{r}_{m}=\pm 1
. This implies that

\displaystyle b_{m}(\pm 1)=-\frac{1}{m}

(25)

for each
m\geq 1.
In the following we always assume
r^{2}<1.

From
Ghosh 1966
or p. 156 in
Muirhead 1982
,

\displaystyle E\big(\hat{r}_{m}^{2})=1-\frac{m-1}{m}(1-r^{2})\cdot{}_{2}F_{1}\Big(1,1;\frac{1}{2}m+1;r^{2}\Big),

(26)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)
is an hypergeometric function defined by

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;z)=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(a)_{k}(b)_{k}}{(c)_{k}k!}z^{k},~~|z|<1,

and where
(x)_{0}=1
and
(x)_{k}=x(x+1)\cdots(x+k-1)
for
k\geq 1
; see, for example, p. 20 from
Muirhead 1982
. Notice
(1)_{k}=k!
and

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}m+1\Big)_{k}=\frac{m+2}{2}\cdot\frac{m+4}{2}\cdots\frac{m+2k}{2}.

Thus

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\Big(1,1;\frac{1}{2}m+1;r^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}k!}{(m+2)(m+4)\cdots(m+2k)}r^{2k}

(27)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+r^{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}k!}{(m+2)(m+4)\cdots(m+2k)}r^{2k-2}.

Evidently, the last sum is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}k!}{(m+2)(m+4)\cdots(m+2k)}.

Set
j=[\frac{m}{2}]
, where
[x]
denotes the integer part of a real number
x\geq 0
. Then
j\leq\frac{m}{2}
, or equivalently,
m\geq 2j
. It follows that

\displaystyle\frac{2^{k}k!}{(m+2)(m+4)\cdots(m+2k)}\leq\frac{k!}{(j+1)(j+2)\cdots(j+k)}=\frac{j!k!}{(j+k)!}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{2^{k}k!}{(m+2)(m+4)\cdots(m+2k)}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\binom{j+k}{j}}\leq\frac{K_{1}}{j^{1/4}}\leq\frac{K_{2}}{m^{1/4}}

as
j\geq 2
, or equivalently,
m\geq 4
by Lemma
6.1
, where
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are constants not depending on
m
. From this and (
27
), we are able to write

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\Big(1,1;\frac{1}{2}m+1;r^{2}\Big)=1+a_{m}r^{2},

where
0\leq a_{m}\leq K_{2}m^{-1/4}.
Substitute this back to (
26
), we get

\displaystyle E\big(\hat{r}_{m}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{m-1}{m}(1-r^{2})\cdot(1+a_{m}r^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{m-1}{m}(1-r^{2})-\frac{m-1}{m}(1-r^{2})\cdot a_{m}r^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m}+r^{2}+b_{m}(r)r^{2},

where

\displaystyle b_{m}(r)=-\frac{1}{m}r^{2}-\frac{m-1}{m}(1-r^{2})\cdot a_{m}r^{2}.

Obviously,

\sup_{|r|\leq 1}|b_{m}(r)|\leq K_{3}m^{-1/4}
for every
m\geq 4
, where
K_{3}
is a constant not depending on
m
. This and (
25
) lead to the desired conclusion.
\square

L
EMMA
6.3

Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{X}}_{n}
be a random sample from
N_{p}({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
with correlation matrix
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\hat{\mathbb{R}}=\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}
be the sample correlation matrix defined in (
7
). If
\lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n\|\mathbb{R}\|_{F}}=0
and
\limsup_{p\to\infty}\frac{p}{n^{a}}=0
for some constant
a>0
, then, as
p\to\infty
,

\displaystyle E\,\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}^{2})=\frac{p(p-1)}{n-1}+\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}_{p}^{2})\cdot\big[1+O(m^{-1/4})\big].

Proof of Lemma
6.3
. Set
m=n-1
. The notation
W_{p}(m,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
represents the distribution of the Wishart matrix
\mathbb{U}^{T}\mathbb{U}
, where
\mathbb{U}
is an
m\times p
matrix whose rows are i.i.d. with distribution
N_{p}(\mathbb{0},{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
. Then,
n\hat{{\bf S}}
has the Wishart distribution
W_{p}(m,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})
; see, for example, Theorem 3.1.2 from
Muirhead 1982
. That is,
n\hat{{\bf S}}\overset{d}{=}\mathbb{U}^{T}\mathbb{U}
. We claim

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}=(\hat{r}_{ij})_{p\times p}\ \overset{d}{=}\Big(\frac{\mathbb{v}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{v}_{j}}{\|\mathbb{v}_{i}^{T}\|\cdot\|\mathbb{v}_{j}\|}\Big)_{p\times p},

(28)

where the
m
rows of
(\mathbb{v}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{v}_{p})_{m\times p}
are i.i.d. with distribution
N_{p}(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{R})
.
In fact, write
\mathbb{U}=(u_{ij})=(\mathbb{u}_{1},\cdots,\mathbb{u}_{p})
where
\mathbb{u}_{i}=(u_{1i},\cdots,u_{mi})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for each
i
. Then
\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}
is the
(i,j)
-entry of
\mathbb{U}^{T}\mathbb{U}
and the diagonal entries are
\|\mathbb{u}_{i}\|^{2}
for
1\leq i\leq p
. We know from (
7
) that

\hat{{\bf R}}_{p}=\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}\hat{{\bf S}}\hat{{\bf D}}^{-1/2}
,
where
\hat{{\bf D}}
is the diagonal matrix of
\hat{{\bf S}}
. Then

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}=(\hat{r}_{ij})_{p\times p}\ \overset{d}{=}\Big(\frac{\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}}{\|\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\|\cdot\|\mathbb{u}_{j}\|}\Big)_{p\times p}.

Notice
(\mathbb{u}_{i},\mathbb{u}_{j})=(u_{ki},u_{kj})_{1\leq k\leq m}
for any
i<j
and the
m
rows are i.i.d. bivariate normal random variables with mean vector
\mathbb{0}
and covariance matrix

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{ii}&\sigma_{ij}\\
\sigma_{ij}&\sigma_{jj}\end{pmatrix}

where
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}:=(\sigma_{ij})_{p\times p}
. Write
\mathbb{R}_{p}=(r_{ij})_{p\times p}
. Then
r_{ij}=\sigma_{ij}(\sigma_{ii}\sigma_{jj})^{-1/2}
by definition. Observe

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbb{u}_{j}}{\|\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\|\cdot\|\mathbb{u}_{j}\|}=\frac{(\sigma_{ii}^{-1/2}\mathbb{u}_{i})^{T}(\sigma_{jj}^{-1/2}\mathbb{u}_{j})}{\|\sigma_{ii}^{-1/2}\mathbb{u}_{i}^{T}\|\cdot\|\sigma_{jj}^{-1/2}\mathbb{u}_{j}\|}.

Take
\mathbb{v}_{i}=\sigma_{ii}^{-1/2}\mathbb{u}_{i}
to obtain (
28
).

By Lemma
6.2
,

\displaystyle E\,\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}E\big(\hat{r}_{ij}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}\Big[\frac{1}{m}+r_{ij}^{2}+b_{m}(r_{ij})r_{ij}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p(p-1)}{m}+\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}_{p}^{2})+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}b_{m}(r_{ij})r_{ij}^{2},

where
\sup_{|r|\leq 1}|b_{m}(r)|\leq Km^{-1/4}
and
K
is a constant not depending on
m
or
r
. Since

\displaystyle 2\Big|\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p}b_{m}(r_{ij})r_{ij}^{2}\Big|\leq(2K)m^{-1/4}\cdot\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}_{p}^{2}),

the above two displays show that

\displaystyle E\,\mbox{tr}(\hat{\mathbb{R}}_{p}^{2})=\frac{p(p-1)}{n-1}+\mbox{tr}(\mathbb{R}_{p}^{2})\cdot\big[1+O(m^{-1/4})\big]

as
p\to\infty.
The proof is completed.
\square

6.2
The Proofs of Theorems
3
and
4

6.2.1
Mixing Moments of Gaussian Random Variables

The following lemma is a very useful tool to compute the mean of the product of Gaussian random variables.

L
EMMA
6.4

(Isserlis and Wick formula) Let
n\geq 2
be an even integer and
(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
follows a normal distribution with mean vector
\mathbb{0}
. Then

\displaystyle E(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\sum_{p}\prod_{(i,j)\in p}E(X_{i}X_{j}),

where the sum runs over every pairing
p
of
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
, that is, all distinct ways of partitioning
\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
into pairs
\{i,j\}
, and the product is over the pairs contained in
p
. Sometimes we also use its equivalent form:

\displaystyle E(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\sum_{i=2}^{n}E(X_{1}X_{i})\cdot E\Big(\frac{X_{2}\cdots X_{n}}{X_{i}}\Big).

A seemingly more general, but actually an equivalent formula of Lemma
6.4
is the following Lemma
6.5
, which will be used only once in a comment after the proof of Lemma
6.7
.

L
EMMA
6.5

(
Guiard 1986
)

Let
X=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})
be n-dimensionally centralized normally distributed with
Cov(X_{i},X_{j})=\sigma_{ij}
. Let
\{\alpha_{i};\,1\leq i\leq n\}
be positive integers.
If
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}
is odd, then
\mathbb{E}(\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})=0.

If
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}
is even, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}\right)=\sum_{2\beta_{ii}+\sum_{j:j\neq i}\beta_{ij}=\alpha_{i},\forall i}\dfrac{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}!}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}2^{\beta_{ii}}\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\beta_{ij}!}\prod_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}\sigma_{ij}^{\beta_{ij}},

where
\{\beta_{ij},1\leq i\leq j\leq n\}
are non-negative integers.

In Lemmas
6.6
and
6.7
below, two identities on mixing moments of Gaussian random variables will be presented.

L
EMMA
6.6

Let
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})^{T}\sim N_{4}(0,\mathbb{R})
. Assume
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})_{4\times 4}
with
r_{ii}=1
for each
i
. Then

(i)
E(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})=r_{12}+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}
;

(ii)
E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2})=1+2r_{12}^{2}+2r_{13}^{2}+2r_{23}^{2}+8r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}.

Proof of Lemma
6.6
.
Let
(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{6})^{T}
be a multivariate normal with mean vector
\mathbb{0}
,
\mbox{Cov}(\xi_{i},\xi_{j})=\sigma_{ij}
and
\mbox{Var}(\xi_{i})=1
for each
i
. Then, by Lemma
6.4
,

\displaystyle E(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}\xi_{4}\xi_{5}\xi_{6})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{12}\sigma_{34}\sigma_{56}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{35}\sigma_{46}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{36}\sigma_{45}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{13}\sigma_{24}\sigma_{56}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{46}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{26}\sigma_{45}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{14}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{56}+\sigma_{14}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{36}+\sigma_{14}\sigma_{26}\sigma_{35}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{46}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{24}\sigma_{36}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{26}\sigma_{34}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{16}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{45}+\sigma_{16}\sigma_{24}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{16}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{34}.

Now take
\xi_{3}=\xi_{4}
and
\xi_{5}=\xi_{6}
to see

\displaystyle E(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}^{2}\xi_{5}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{12}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{35}^{2}+\sigma_{12}\sigma_{35}^{2}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{13}\sigma_{23}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{35}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{13}\sigma_{23}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{35}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{25}+

\displaystyle\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{35}+\sigma_{15}\sigma_{25}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma_{12}+2\sigma_{12}\sigma_{35}^{2}+2\sigma_{13}\sigma_{23}+2\sigma_{15}\sigma_{25}+4\sigma_{13}\sigma_{25}\sigma_{35}+4\sigma_{15}\sigma_{23}\sigma_{35}.

This says that

\displaystyle E(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})=r_{12}+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}.

(29)

We obtain (i). Now, take
X_{2}=X_{1}
in (
29
). By using the fact
r_{12}=1
and by changing “
2
” to “
1
” to the indices of
r
we obtain

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+2r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}^{2}+2r_{14}^{2}+4r_{13}r_{14}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{13}r_{34}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+2r_{13}^{2}+2r_{14}^{2}+2r_{34}^{2}+8r_{13}r_{14}r_{34}.

In the above and change “
3
” to “
2
” and “
4
” to “
3
” to see

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2})=1+2r_{12}^{2}+2r_{13}^{2}+2r_{23}^{2}+8r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}.

The proof is completed.
\square

L
EMMA
6.7

Suppose the 4-dimensional random vector
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})^{T}\sim N_{4}(0,\mathbb{R})
. Assume
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})_{4\times 4}
with
r_{ii}=1
for each
i
. Then

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}r_{ij}^{2}+4(r_{12}^{2}r_{34}^{2}+r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2})

\displaystyle\ \ +8(r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{41}+r_{23}r_{34}r_{42}+r_{13}r_{34}r_{41})

\displaystyle\ \ +16(r_{12}r_{23}r_{34}r_{41}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{43}r_{31}+r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}).

In particular, take
X_{3}=X_{1}
and
X_{4}=X_{2}
to see
E(X_{1}^{4}X_{2}^{4})=9+72r_{12}^{2}+24r_{12}^{4}.

Proof of Lemma
6.7
. By Lemma
6.4
,

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2})\cdot E(X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})+2E(X_{1}X_{2})\cdot E(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle+2E(X_{1}X_{3})\cdot E(X_{1}X_{3}X_{2}^{2}X_{4}^{2})+2E(X_{1}X_{4})\cdot E(X_{1}X_{4}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}).

By Lemma
6.6
(i),

\displaystyle E(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})=r_{12}+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}.

(30)

Similarly, we obtain
E(X_{1}X_{3}X_{2}^{2}X_{4}^{2})
and
E(X_{1}X_{4}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2})
by exchanging the roles of “
X_{2}
” and “
X_{3}
” and exchanging the roles of “
X_{2}
” and “
X_{4}
”, respectively, from (
30
). By Lemma
6.6
(ii),

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2})=1+2r_{12}^{2}+2r_{13}^{2}+2r_{23}^{2}+8r_{12}r_{13}r_{23}.

Hence,

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+2r_{23}^{2}+2r_{24}^{2}+2r_{34}^{2}+8r_{23}r_{24}r_{34}+

\displaystyle 2r_{12}\big(r_{12}+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}\big)+

\displaystyle 2r_{13}\big(r_{13}+2r_{13}r_{24}^{2}+2r_{12}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{34}+4r_{12}r_{34}r_{24}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{24}\big)+

\displaystyle 2r_{14}\big(r_{14}+2r_{14}r_{23}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{34}+2r_{12}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{23}+4r_{12}r_{34}r_{23}\big).

Sorting them out, we have

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+2\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}r_{ij}^{2}+4(r_{12}^{2}r_{34}^{2}+r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2})+

\displaystyle 8(r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{41}+r_{23}r_{34}r_{42}+r_{13}r_{34}r_{41})+

\displaystyle 16(r_{12}r_{23}r_{34}r_{41}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{43}r_{31}+r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}).

The proof is completed.
\square

Lemma
6.7
studies
E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}\cdots X_{n}^{2})
for
n=4
, which is sufficient for our purpose. It is interesting to see the formula for
n\geq 5
by pure curiosity. If we argue the same way as in the proof of Lemma
6.7
through Lemma
6.4
, the sorting procedure would be messy. However, Lemma
6.5
provides a way to do so by figuring out the non-negative integer solutions of the system equations
2\beta_{ii}+\sum_{j:j\neq i}\beta_{ij}=2
for
1\leq i\leq n.

The following is a key step to study the covariance between two squared sample covariances stated in Lemma
6.9
.

L
EMMA
6.8

Let
\{(X_{1j},X_{2j},X_{3j},X_{4j})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{4};\,1\leq j\leq m\}
be i.i.d. random vectors with distribution
N_{4}(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathbb{R}=(r_{ij})_{4\times 4}
and
r_{ii}=1
for each
i
. Set

\displaystyle B_{1}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}X_{1j}X_{2j}\ \ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \ B_{2}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}X_{3j}X_{4j}.

Then,

(i)
\mbox{Var}(B_{1})=\frac{1}{m}(1+r_{12}^{2})
;

(ii)
\mbox{Cov}(B_{1},B_{2})=\frac{1}{m}(r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23})
;

(iii)
E[(B_{1}-r_{12})(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=\frac{2}{m^{2}}(r_{13}r_{23}+r_{14}r_{24}+r_{13}r_{34}r_{42}+r_{14}r_{43}r_{32})
;

(iv)
E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]
is identical to

\displaystyle\ \ \frac{1}{m^{2}}(1+r_{12}^{2})(1+r_{34}^{2})+\frac{2}{m^{2}}\big[r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}\big]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{m^{3}}\big[(r_{13}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}+r_{23}^{2}+r_{24}^{2})+(r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2})

\displaystyle+2(r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{41}+r_{23}r_{34}r_{42}+r_{13}r_{34}r_{41})

\displaystyle+2r_{12}r_{23}r_{34}r_{41}+2r_{12}r_{24}r_{43}r_{31}+6r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}\big].

Comment
. We now conduct an independent check of the accuracy of Lemma
6.8
(iv) for two special cases. In the above result, take
X_{1j}=X_{2j}=X_{3j}=X_{4j}
, then
r_{ij}=1
for all
i,j
. Consequently, we get from Theorem
6.8
(iv) that

\displaystyle E\Big[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(X_{1j}^{2}-1)\Big]^{4}=E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=\frac{12}{m^{2}}+\frac{48}{m^{3}}.

Notice
\{X_{1j};\,1\leq j\leq m\}
are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
. Then
\sum_{j=1}^{m}(X_{1j}^{2}-1)
is a sum of i.i.d. random variables with
E(X_{11}^{2}-1)^{2}=2
and
E(X_{11}^{2}-1)^{4}=60
. By a classical formula (see, for example, p. 69 from
Durrett 2019
),

\displaystyle E\Big[\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(X_{1j}^{2}-1)\Big]^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{4}}\big\{m\cdot E(X_{11}^{2}-1)^{4}+3m(m-1)\cdot\big[E(X_{11}^{2}-1)^{2}\big]^{2}\big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{12}{m^{2}}+\frac{48}{m^{3}}.

So Theorem
6.8
(iv) recovers the case for
r_{ij}=1
for all
i,j
. On the other hand, assume the two
2
-dimensional random vectors
(X_{11}\,X_{21})^{T}
and
(X_{31}\,X_{41})^{T}
are independent, that is,
r_{13}=r_{14}=r_{23}=r_{24}=0
. By Theorem
6.8
(iv),

\displaystyle E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=\frac{1}{m^{2}}(1+r_{12}^{2})(1+r_{34}^{2}).

On the other hand, by independence and Theorem
6.8
(i),

\displaystyle E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}]\cdot E[(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=\frac{1}{m^{2}}(1+r_{12}^{2})(1+r_{34}^{2}).

So this independent check indicates that Theorem
6.8
(iv) holds for the case
r_{13}=r_{14}=r_{23}=r_{24}=0
.

Proof of Lemma
6.8
. First, by Lemma
6.4
,

\displaystyle E(X_{11}^{2}X_{21}^{2})=1+2r_{12}^{2},\ \ E(X_{11}^{3}X_{21})=3r_{12},

(31)

\displaystyle E(X_{11}^{2}X_{21}X_{31})=r_{23}+2r_{12}r_{13},\ \ E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}X_{41})=r_{12}r_{34}+r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

(32)

In fact, the two middle identities from the four in the above can be deduced immediately from the last one by taking
X_{11}=X_{21}=X_{31}
and
X_{11}=X_{21}
, respectively.

(i) Notice
mB_{1}
and
mB_{2}
are sums of i.i.d. random variables with mean
r_{12}
and
r_{34}
, respectively. Also,
X_{ij}\sim N(0,1)
for each
i,j.
Then

E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}]=\frac{1}{m}\mbox{Var}(X_{11}X_{21})
. Since
\mbox{Cov}(X_{11},X_{21})=E(X_{11}X_{21})=r_{12}
, we have from (
31
) that
\mbox{Var}(X_{11}X_{21})=1+r_{12}^{2}
.
So (i) follows.

(ii) By independence,
E[(B_{1}-r_{12})(B_{2}-r_{34})]=\frac{1}{m}\mbox{Cov}(X_{11}X_{21},X_{31}X_{41}).
From (
32
),

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}(X_{11}X_{21},X_{31}X_{41})=r_{12}r_{34}+r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23}-r_{12}r_{34}=r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23}.

(33)

(iii) Write

\displaystyle(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(X_{3j}X_{4j}-r_{34})^{2}+\frac{2}{m^{2}}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq m}(X_{3k}X_{4k}-r_{34})(X_{3l}X_{4l}-r_{34}).

(34)

By independence, the covariance between
B_{1}-r_{12}
and any term from the last sum is zero. This implies

\displaystyle E[(B_{1}-r_{12})(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{2}}E[(X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})^{2}].

(35)

Use expansion
(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})^{2}=X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2}-2r_{34}X_{31}X_{41}+r_{34}^{2}
to see that
the last expectation in (
35
) is equal to
E[(X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})(X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2}-2r_{34}X_{31}X_{41})]
, which is again equal to

\displaystyle E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2})-2r_{34}E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}X_{41})-r_{12}E(X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2})+2r_{12}r_{34}E(X_{31}X_{41})

(36)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2})-2r_{34}(r_{12}r_{34}+r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23})-r_{12}(1+2r_{34}^{2})+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2})-2r_{34}(r_{12}r_{34}+r_{13}r_{24}+r_{14}r_{23})-r_{12}

by (
31
) and (
32
). From Lemma
6.6
(i), we see that

\displaystyle E(X_{11}X_{21}X_{31}^{2}X_{41}^{2})=r_{12}+2r_{12}r_{34}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}.

(37)

Plug this into the previous display,we arrive at

\displaystyle E[(B_{1}-r_{12})(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\big(2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+2r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+2r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}\big).

(iv) By (i),

\displaystyle E[(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}]

(38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mbox{Cov}\big((B_{1}-r_{12})^{2},(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}\big)+E(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}\cdot E(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\mbox{Cov}\big((B_{1}-r_{12})^{2},(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}\big)+\frac{1}{m^{2}}(1+r_{12}^{2})(1+r_{34}^{2}).

Similar to (
34
), we have

\displaystyle(B_{1}-r_{12})^{2}=\frac{1}{m^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(X_{1j}X_{2j}-r_{12})^{2}+\frac{2}{m^{2}}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq m}(X_{1k}X_{2k}-r_{12})(X_{1l}X_{2l}-r_{12}).

(39)

Recall the
m
random variables
\{(X_{1j},X_{2j},X_{3j},X_{4j})^{T};\,1\leq j\leq m\}
are i.i.d., thus each term from the double sums in (
34
) and (
39
) is a product of two independent random variables. This implies that
E[(X_{3k}X_{4k}-r_{34})(X_{3l}X_{4l}-r_{34})]=0
for any
k<l
, and the term
(X_{1j}X_{2j}-r_{12})^{2}
is uncorrelated to
(X_{3k}X_{4k}-r_{34})(X_{3l}X_{4l}-r_{34})
for any
j,k,l
with
k<l.
By the same spirit, it is easy to check that
(X_{1k}X_{2k}-r_{12})(X_{1l}X_{2l}-r_{12})
and
(X_{3k_{1}}X_{4k_{1}}-r_{34})(X_{3l_{1}}X_{4l_{1}}-r_{34})
are uncorrelated for any
k<l
and
k_{1}<l_{1}
as long as
(k,l)\neq(k_{1},l_{1})
. These yield

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\big((B_{1}-r_{12})^{2},(B_{2}-r_{34})^{2}\big)

(40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{3}}\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})^{2},(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})^{2}\big)+

\displaystyle\frac{4}{m^{4}}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq m}\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})(X_{12}X_{22}-r_{12}),(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})(X_{32}X_{42}-r_{34})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{m^{3}}\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})^{2},(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})^{2}\big)+

\displaystyle\frac{2(m-1)}{m^{3}}\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})(X_{12}X_{22}-r_{12}),(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})(X_{32}X_{42}-r_{34})\big).

For brevity of notation, let
(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3},Y_{4})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
be i.i.d. random vectors with distribution
N_{4}(\mathbb{0},\mathbb{R}).
Then the last covariance in (
40
) is identical to

\displaystyle E\big[(X_{1}X_{2}-r_{12})(Y_{1}Y_{2}-r_{12})(X_{3}X_{4}-r_{34})(Y_{3}Y_{4}-r_{34})\big]

(41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{E[(X_{1}X_{2}-r_{12})(X_{3}X_{4}-r_{34})]\big\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2}+2r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}

by independence and (
33
). Now we calculate the first covariance in (
40
). In fact,

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{11}X_{21}-r_{12})^{2},(X_{31}X_{41}-r_{34})^{2}\big)

(42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\big((X_{1}X_{2}-r_{12})^{2},(X_{3}X_{4}-r_{34})^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\big(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2},X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})-2r_{12}\mbox{Cov}\big(X_{1}X_{2},X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})-2r_{34}\mbox{Cov}\big(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2},X_{3}X_{4})+

\displaystyle 4r_{12}r_{34}\mbox{Cov}\big(X_{1}X_{2},X_{3}X_{4}\big)

since
(X_{1}X_{2}-r_{12})^{2}=X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}-2r_{12}X_{1}X_{2}+r_{12}^{2}
and
(X_{3}X_{4}-r_{34})^{2}=X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2}-2r_{34}X_{3}X_{4}+r_{34}^{2}
. From (
31
) and Lemma
6.7
,

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2},X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2}X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})-E(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2})\cdot E(X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(r_{13}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}+r_{23}^{2}+r_{24}^{2})+4(r_{13}^{2}r_{24}^{2}+r_{14}^{2}r_{23}^{2})

\displaystyle+8(r_{12}r_{23}r_{31}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{41}+r_{23}r_{34}r_{42}+r_{13}r_{34}r_{41})

\displaystyle+16(r_{12}r_{23}r_{34}r_{41}+r_{12}r_{24}r_{43}r_{31}+r_{13}r_{32}r_{24}r_{41}).

Also, by (
37
),

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\,(X_{1}X_{2},X_{3}^{2}X_{4}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}^{3}X_{4}^{2})-r_{12}(1+2r_{34}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2r_{13}r_{23}+2r_{14}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{34}+4r_{14}r_{23}r_{34}.

By exchanging “
1
” to “
3
” and exchanging “
2
” and “
4
” in the above, we obtain

\displaystyle\mbox{Cov}\,(X_{1}^{2}X_{2}^{2},X_{3}X_{4})=2r_{13}r_{14}+2r_{23}r_{24}+4r_{13}r_{24}r_{12}+4r_{23}r_{14}r_{12}.
